Therefore He comes personally to teach us how to return to Him.” (*The Journey of Self-Discovery,* Chapter 1). So are we gloomies or happies? Without carefree-ness and recreation, can one be happy?

Are we sad when pondering the “karma versus compassion” question? Do we wring our hands in distress, or are we blissfully aloof, chalking it all up to karma (“they got what they deserved”). Was Prahlāda Maharaja—a pure devotee—“*always* \[emphasis mine\] aggrieved to see others’ suffering” (*Śrīmad-Bhāgavatam* 7.9, chapter summary)?

Somehow, Śrīla Prabhupāda, you were always happy, but you must also have been sad.

There is no doubt that you cared about the billions in the world who suffer. But how can one be happy and sad at the same time? You partly answered this question in a *Śrīmad-Bhāgavatam* lecture you gave on June 15, 1972, intimating there are two kinds of happiness:

When partition was made in India, all poor Hindus and Muslims, they were in trouble. And the leaders, they were in happy mood in their apartment, ordering and eating very nicely, butter and bread. This is going on. The only opportunity of becoming happy is that you preach this Kṛṣṇa consciousness movement amongst the people. .

In a 1969 New York talk, you reminded your audience, us, that “real occupational duty is to render service to others.”

You repeatedly stressed that a *madhyama-adhikārī* is one who sees all living entities as ignorant and childlike and in desperate need of Kṛṣṇa consciousness.

But preaching for us neophytes often takes the form of passion, wanting to do something because we seem to be forever active; we desperately need something to do. We need a purpose or a cause; we’ve inherited a restlessness that’s part of our destiny. Our compassion is as off-hand as a Hallmark card. Do we attend funerals just to get them over with? Do we really care, or do we just need something to do? Is what drives us really our own need, or is it selfless concern for others?

You indicated to us it was OK to start selfishly—kind of backwards. Start doing something for suffering humanity and then gradually feel the misfortunes of others as one’s own.

If empathy is the substance and preaching is the form, then maybe it’s OK that in the beginning we don’t care, or don’t care very much—maybe it’s OK that we just *want* to care, *want* to get there. I think that if I strive to attain this feeling, I can protect my attempts at devotional service from lapsing into karma and other mundane activity. As the *Gītā* tells us (15.7), we’re all related through a common Father.

You asked us not to see in terms of our “own men.” In 1974 you said,

What is that “own men”? “Own men” means this bodily relationship. Why others are not own men? Everyone is own men. Because everyone is Kṛṣṇa’s son. So when one becomes Kṛṣṇa conscious, he can see everyone own men. And when he is not Kṛṣṇa conscious, he simply sees own men where there is bodily relationship. This is the defect.

And in another lecture you said that “One who is actually learned in Kṛṣṇa consciousness becomes a lover of everyone in the universe.” And you wrote that “Unless one is situated on the Kṛṣṇa conscious platform, there is no question of universal brotherhood.”

Haridāsa Ṭhākura was born a Muslim in the latter part of the fifteenth century in Bangladesh. He literally felt the searing pain on his own back as the suffering of those who tried unsuccessfully to beat him to death with wooden branches. He knew the tormentors would die at the hands of their master if they didn’t kill him. Miraculously alive after the beatings, he feigned death to save his would-be killers from execution.

Another extreme case is that of Vāsudeva Datta, who expressed his wish to accept the karmic reactions of every living entity in the universe. He wanted all beings to be freed from their karmic retribution (*Caitanya-caritāmṛta, Madhya* 15.163). In your purport to this verse you compare Vāsudeva Datta and Śrīla Haridāsa Ṭhākura with Lord Jesus Christ:

We find Śrī Vāsudeva Datta Ṭhākura and Śrīla Haridāsa Ṭhākura to be many millions of times more advanced even when compared with Lord Jesus Christ. Jesus Christ relieved only his followers from all sinful reactions, but Vāsudeva Datta is here prepared to accept the sins of everyone in the universe. So the comparative position of Vāsudeva Datta is millions of times better than that of Lord Jesus Christ. A Vaiṣṇava is so liberal that he is prepared to risk everything to rescue the conditioned souls from material existence. Vāsudeva Datta is universal love itself, for he was willing to sacrifice everything and fully engage in the service of the Supreme Lord.

Knowledge, many think, is the way to freedom. But freedom does not mean social mobility; nor is it about our choices of garment colors, banks, cars, computers, cell phones, lifestyles, mates and friends, or places to visit or live. It means freedom to know that friendship, society, and love are not our ultimate source of pleasure. Freedom is to know our real identity as souls who serve God (*jīvera ‘svarūpa’ haya*—*kṛṣṇera ‘nitya-dāsa’*\[Cc *Madhya* 20.108\]). Teachers and preachers of this knowledge are the world’s finest welfare workers.

That makes you, Śrīla Prabhupāda, the world’s greatest philanthropist, the man of the century, the man of the millennium.

*Your servant,*  
Mukunda Goswami

## Paraṁ Gati Swami

*nama oṁ viṣṇu-pādāya kṛṣṇa-preṣṭhāya bhū-tale*  
*śrīmate bhaktivedānta-svāminn iti nāmine*

*namas te sārasvate deve gaura-vāṇī-pracāriṇe*  
*nirviśeṣa-śūnyavādi-pāścātya-deśa-tāriṇe*

My dear Śrīla Prabhupāda,

Please accept my humble and respectful obeisances unto your lotus feet.

The ability to maintain enthusiasm and determination in a preaching mission, even while facing great obstacles, is one of the characteristics of a *paramahaṁsa*. At the same time, a *paramahaṁsa* is grave and introspective so that he may always act according to time, place, and circumstance, and consequently he speaks Vāsudeva’s message in a way that anyone can accept it. Due to your complete dependence on and total surrender to the Supreme Lord Kṛṣṇa, you possess these qualities at the highest level.

While fulfilling your preaching mission you gave us innumerable practical examples of your humbleness and other exalted qualities mentioned in the *śāstra,* which guaranteed your success as a preacher.

The Kṛṣṇa consciousness movement will remain on this planet and help humanity for the next 10,000 years. Yet you have already revealed the secret of success: If we simply keep your clear instructions in our hearts, then both our spiritual lives and the preaching mission will be successful, and all errors, tragedies, and consequent lamentation and pain will be minimized.

I earnestly pray unto your lotus feet that I can understand this point more and more deeply and thus continue assisting your mission as a useful instrument within ISKCON.

*Your worthless servant,*  
Paraṁ Gati Swami

## Prabhaviṣṇu Swami

Dear Śrīla Prabhupāda,

Please accept my most humble obeisances at your lotus feet. All glories to Your Divine Grace!

Thank you so much for all that you have given us—Kṛṣṇa, Caitanya Mahāprabhu, the holy name, the sacred scriptures, the association of wonderful devotees, so many beautiful Deities and temples, delicious *kṛṣṇa-prasādam,* unlimited opportunity for engagement in devotional service. The list of all these, and so much more, could go on and on ad infinitum. If you had not felt deep concern for the ignorant masses, or had not taken the trouble to leave your beloved Vṛndāvana to go and preach in the West, we would never have had the chance to understand anything about spiritual life. However, by your personal sacrifice and potent instructions vast spiritual horizons have been opened for innumerable living entities. What limitless mercy and compassion you have shown to us fallen souls! We had no qualification whatsoever, yet you have made Kṛṣṇa available to us for eternity.

I pray that I may not waste this opportunity, this priceless treasure that you have given us. From the bottom of my heart I thank you again and again, Śrīla Prabhupāda. May your fame be spread all over the three worlds.

*Your insignificant servant,*  
Prabhaviṣṇu Swami

## Praghoṣa dāsa

**“With Your Words”**

I get so lost sometimes  
When emptiness fills my heart  
Wanting to run away  
But I know the only place I’ll find relief  
Is under the shelter of your lotus feet

Then my intelligence starts to return  
And the grand facade begins to burn  
Without a reason, without my deserving  
You reach out and touch me inside

With your words  
I see the light, I feel the heat  
With your words  
I hear the truth, illusion beat  
With your words  
I see the doorway to a fearless future  
With your words  
I see the resolution of all fruitless searches

But still my mind is urging me to explore  
so much wasting time, time that is slipping away  
I get so frustrated with my lack of urgency  
Failing again in what I should do  
Until I immerse myself in thoughts of you

Then my intelligence starts to return  
And the grand facade begins to burn  
Without a reason, without my deserving  
You reach out and touch me inside

With your words  
I see the light, I feel the heat  
With your words  
I hear the truth, illusion beat  
With your words  
I see the doorway to a fearless future  
With your words  
I see the resolution of all fruitless searches

*Your servant,*  
Praghoṣa dāsa

## Prithu dāsa Adhikārī

*oṁ ajñāna-timirāndhasya jñānāñjana-śalākayā*  
*cakṣur unmīlitaṁ yena tasmai śrī-gurave namaḥ*

*nama oṁ viṣṇu-pādāya kṛṣṇa-preṣṭhāya bhū-tale*  
*śrīmate bhaktivedānta-svāminn iti nāmine*

*namas te sārasvate deve gaura-vāṇī-pracāriṇe*  
*nirviśeṣa-śūnyavādi-pāścātya-deśa-tāriṇe*

Dear Śrīla Prabhupāda,

Please accept my humble obeisances.

Your faith in Kṛṣṇa and your spiritual master, Śrīla Bhaktisiddhānta Sarasvatī, is unfathomable.

*yasya deve parā bhaktir yathā deve tathā gurau*  
*tasyaite kathitā hy arthāḥ prakāśante mahātmanaḥ*

**“Only to one who has staunch faith in both the words of the spiritual master and those of the Supreme Personality of Godhead are the secrets of Vedic knowledge revealed.” (*****Śvetāśvatara Upaniṣad*** **6.23)**

You were so dedicated to following the instructions of Śrīla Bhaktisiddhānta Sarasvatī Ṭhākura. Hence you derived incredible results:

*vyavasāyātmikā buddhir ekeha kuru-nandana*  
*bahu-śākhā hy anantāś ca buddhayo ’vyavasāyinām*

**“Those who are on this path are resolute in purpose, and their aim is one. O beloved child of the Kurus, the intelligence of those who are irresolute is many-branched.” (*****Bhagavad-gītā*** **2.41)**

You say in the purport:

A person in Kṛṣṇa consciousness is the rare good soul who knows perfectly that Vāsudeva, or Kṛṣṇa, is the root of all manifested causes. As by watering the root of a tree one automatically distributes water to the leaves and branches, so by acting in Kṛṣṇa consciousness one can render the highest service to everyone—namely self, family, society, country, humanity, etc. If Kṛṣṇa is satisfied by one’s actions, then everyone will be satisfied.

Śrīla Viśvanātha Cakravartī Ṭhākura, in his prayers to the spiritual master, similarly states:

*yasya prasādād bhagavat-prasādo*  
*yasyāprasādān na gatiḥ kuto ’pi*  
*dhyāyan stuvaṁs tasya yaśas tri-sandhyaṁ*  
*vande guroḥ śrī-caraṇāravindam*

**“By satisfaction of the spiritual master, the Supreme Personality of Godhead becomes satisfied. And by not satisfying the spiritual master, there is no chance of being promoted to the plane of Kṛṣṇa consciousness. I should, therefore, meditate and pray for his mercy three times a day, and offer my respectful obeisances unto him, my spiritual master.”**

You indeed satisfied your Guru Mahārāja by being cent percent obedient to his mission. Others fell away or followed their own ideas. You had full faith and achieved incomparable results. It’s very clear: You had Kṛṣṇa on your side.

*kali-kālera dharma—kṛṣṇa-nāma-saṅkīrtana*  
*kṛṣṇa-śakti vinā nahe tāra pravartana*

**“The fundamental religious system in the Age of Kali is the chanting of the holy name of Kṛṣṇa. Unless empowered by Kṛṣṇa, one cannot propagate the saṅkīrtana movement.” (*****Caitanya-caritāmṛta, Antya*** **7.11)**

Please keep dragging this useless soul along. May I also please you one day. Begging for your mercy,

*Your poor disciple,*  
Prithu dāsa Adhikārī

## Rāmāi Swami

Dear Śrīla Prabhupāda,

Please accept my humble obeisances. All glories unto you!

In the *Śrīmad-Bhāgavatam* (3.6.36) Maitreya Muni says to Vidura,

*tathāpi kīrtayāmy aṅga yathā-mati yathā-śrutam*  
*kīrtiṁ hareḥ svāṁ sat-kartuṁ giram anyābhidhāsatīm*

**“In spite of my inability, whatever I have been able to hear \[from the spiritual master\] and whatever I could assimilate I am now describing in glorification of the Lord by pure speech, for otherwise my power of speaking would remain unchaste.”**

In your purport you write, “This glorification of the Lord is not a product of research, but the result of hearing submissively from the authority of the spiritual master. It is also not possible to repeat all that one has heard from his spiritual master, but one can narrate as far as possible by one’s honest endeavor.”

Recently I have begun hearing your taped lectures on a regular basis. I have talked to Godbrothers who do they same thing, and we all agree that to hear from you in this way is one of the most satisfying ways a disciple can hear from the spiritual master.

I remember sitting in some of those lectures, and when I hear them again I am immediately transported back in time to that place. I continue to marvel at how, when you speak, your lectures are so potent and structured that it seems you must have planned them beforehand. Your knowledge appears unlimited. You quote so many *ślokas* in such an authorized way.

You were not afraid to speak the absolute truth in spite of the circumstances, but you did it in such a way that everyone appreciated what you were saying. Such is the power of a pure devotee of the Lord. When will I ever become a pure devotee of the Lord? Now I find that the more I hear in this way, the more I repeat your words and the more people appreciate what I say. This I find most pleasing.

Your humor is also very wonderful. Sometimes I tell some of your stories, and although you spoke them thirty years ago I find that people still find the humor and moral very appealing. As your disciples, we are trying to follow in your footsteps, but your footsteps are very big. How do we properly follow you? We need your guidance and mercy to understand this.

I find that many disciples have their own idea as to how to spread this Kṛṣṇa consciousness movement, but I think we have to follow your ideas. You gave us enough instructions to do what is needed. Let us simply stick to them. We don’t have to concoct or make things complicated. You are the most advanced and sophisticated devotee of the Lord, but one of your most attractive qualities is your transcendental simplicity.

“Don’t centralize,” “Don’t make and then break,” “Use your common sense,” “Don’t be over-intelligent,” you used to say. I always try to remember these instructions, but in the course of time things sometimes become blurred. We are limited *jīvas* under the influence of the material energy in Kali-yuga. We need to always hold tightly to your lotus feet and never let go. Please do not kick us away because of our offensive nature. You are the only means of our salvation and elevation to Kṛṣṇa’s lotus feet.

*Your insignificant servant,*  
Rāmāi Swami

## Romapāda Swami

*nama oṁ viṣṇu-pādāya kṛṣṇa-preṣṭhāya bhū-tale*  
*śrīmate bhaktivedānta-svāminn iti nāmine*

*namas te sārasvate deve gaura-vāṇī-pracāriṇe*  
*nirviśeṣa-śūnyavādi-pāścātya-deśa-tāriṇe*

*mūkaṁ karoti vācālaṁ paṅguṁ laṅghayate girim*  
*yat-kṛpā tam ahaṁ vande śrī-guruṁ dīna-tāraṇam*

My most dear spiritual master, most merciful Śrīla Prabhupāda,

The occasion of your Vyāsa-pūjā is upon us once again, providing all your followers and disciples worldwide with the opportunity to express our unbridled praises of your unlimited glories! Such concentrated meditation upon you on this occasion is simply wonderful! Your purity is being felt by us all, most intensely.

Śrīla Prabhupāda, you are the most wonderful loving father one could ever dream of! In abundance, you have freely given us absolutely everything we need to traverse the path of *bhakti,* from its most infantile beginnings to its full conclusion. You found us loitering in some dark place, you ignited our most basic *śraddhā,* and you are prepared to carry us to fully blossomed *prema,* despite our disqualifications. Your mercy is awesome, and your capacity to deliver all who contact you likewise knows no limit.

To insure that we reached the goal of life, you taught us repeatedly how to identify and avoid the many dangers on the path of devotion. Through these same teachings, you are offering us full protection—if only we take shelter of your ample cautions!

I find you to be such a loving father that you go *way* beyond this in providing us protection. A letter you wrote to Kṛṣṇa dāsa in 1972 has been a steady beacon of inspiration for me. You wrote:

You mentioned that your pathway has become filled with stumbling blocks, but there are no stumbling blocks. I can kick out all those stumbling blocks immediately, provided you accept my guidance. With one stroke of my kick I can kick out all stumbling blocks.

You were ready, willing, and completely able to fulfill this promise. Haven’t we all experienced it?

Beyond that, even, you extended your personal association to your dear and struggling disciple Kṛṣṇa dasa, despite your enormous service and loaded schedule. You continued in your letter to him:

You are always welcome. All of your doubts will be cleared up either personally face-to-face or by correspondence with me. You can come to live with me and all your doubts will \[be\] erased. In the beginning there were no doubts, but by bad association you have now got doubts. I did not give up. I am doing my duty and always keeping my spiritual master in front. Even there is some difficulty or hardship, still I must perform my duty to my spiritual master and not become discouraged and go away. That is my weakness.

By your own example and by your love for each and every one of us, you bring us close to you, and you forever invitingly keep us by your side, in the cooling shade of your caring, loving, personal association. Above all the other protections I have just recognized your having provided us, I find this to be your greatest protection: the ever-growing personal feeling of your presence. This is the best of all kindnesses and protections that you offer.

Who in this world knows that feeling, the feeling of being spiritually loved and causelessly cared for, in all circumstances of life? Only one who is *most* fortunate! I am feeling the continued good fortune of your presence literally growing within my life, and I am absolutely grateful to you for this gift.

I remember very vividly one lecture you gave in the Brooklyn temple about your spiritual master making you fortunate, how you in turn had made us fortunate, and then you sent us all into a state of frenzy by making a fervent appeal that we assist you by extending ourselves to make others likewise fortunate. Please allow me to assist you, for the rest of my life, in fulfilling this request, to whatever capacity you bestow upon me.

As I am writing this offering to you, Śrīla Prabhupāda, here in a remote part of India on a littered railway platform late at night, a child’s shrill crying pierces the night air, muffled by the din of hundreds of indistinct chattering voices of travelers awaiting their commuter train to take them back home after a long day’s labor. Pressed to the mother’s body by one arm, the small child flails his arms and legs as his mother hurries swiftly down the platform, driven by some purpose-filled service for her child.

Were we not also brought into this world by a mother and a father, who also carried us here and there and fed us and schooled us very carefully and purposefully, driven by parental affection? But which one of us was given the gift of understanding the deeper, ultimate purpose of all these varied acts, the higher purpose of being awarded the human form of life? Neither this purpose-driven mother, nor the thousands of fatigued commuters, nor most of the millions and billions of conditioned souls in this world have a clue about this important spiritual understanding. This awareness is your exclusive gift, Śrīla Prabhupāda, the gift of such a qualified and merciful spiritual father. What inspired you to provide us this gift, at great personal inconvenience? Your kindness upon us was *not* driven by bodily affection, nor any of the multitude of motivations or emotions connected to this temporary circumstance of life. It was your causelessly merciful nature.

Moved entirely by spiritual compassion and love, you came into our lives to deliver us completely from illusion and its endless suffering. As much as this gesture of yours was one of pure love, so was your message.

To complete the package of your wonderful gift to us, Śrīla Prabhupāda, you gave us the message of *bhakti* in its pure form. Unlike others who claimed to teach *bhakti,* you explained that the path of *bhakti* is not just devotion but devotional service.

You trained us to pray to Kṛṣṇa by calling His holy name, requesting fervently to be engaged by His internal potency in Kṛṣṇa’s devotional service. This is the key! You taught us to act selflessly, always serving others, in a humble mood, entirely for the satisfaction of Kṛṣṇa. What is called love in this world is invariably tainted with other purposes. Any other purpose than serving Kṛṣṇa, striving to increase His pleasure, will bring our downfall, inviting illusion and bewilderment and its accompanying sufferings.

*yayā sammohito jīva ātmānaṁ tri-guṇātmakam*  
*paro ’pi manute ’narthaṁ tat-kṛtaṁ cābhipadyate*

**“Due to this external energy, the living entity, although transcendental to the three modes of material nature, thinks of himself as a material product and thus undergoes the reactions of material miseries.” (*****Śrīmad-Bhāgavatam*** **1.7.5)**

Only when our single-minded attention goes to Kṛṣṇa, while we serve others in a humble mood, will we be able to experience your message’s full potency in our life. On your Vyāsa-pūjā day, Śrīla Prabhupāda, I once again renew my commitment to you to strive for this standard of devotional service. In this way, I consciously and fully re-dedicate myself to you, and to your mission of propagating pure devotional service.

*Your eternal servant,*  
Romapāda Swami

## Satsvarūpa dāsa Goswami

*nama oṁ viṣṇu-pādāya kṛṣṇa-preṣṭhāya bhū-tale*  
*śrīmate bhaktivedānta-svāminn iti nāmine*

*namas te sārasvate deve gaura-vāṇī-pracāriṇe*  
*nirviśeṣa-śūnyavādi-pāścātya-deśa-tāriṇe*

**Śrīla Prabhupāda as Human and Transcendental**

The Vyāsa-pūjā homages in this book may be described by the literary term *hagiography*. The definition of *hagiography* differs according to one’s viewpoint. For religious people, the meaning is as follows:

*Hagiography* comes from the Greek word *hagios,* meaning “saint.” It refers to the special events in the life of a person which qualify her or him to be a saint. In the early Church, the title “saint” had a technically precise meaning of someone who was in heaven with Christ, could intercede for people, and merited public recognition by the Church. (*The Way to Love: Therese of Lisieux,* by N. LaForest, O.C.D.)

Collins Concise English dictionary defines *hagiography* as “a biography that idealizes or idolizes its subject.” Collins gives a similar word, *hagiology,* or “literature concerned with the lives and legends of saints.” By the use of words like “idealizes” and “legends,” we see that the secular mind tends to think that the descriptions and praises of saints or great devotees, when made by believers, may not necessarily be true and are indeed often exaggerated. Academic scholars and nonbelievers in general tend to assume this attitude. These persons demand what they consider an objective picture of the great devotee, and they usually reject any event in the saint’s life that approaches the miraculous. They prefer their saints whittled down.

Of course, we don’t care for the definitions of the nondevotees, yet we should strive for honesty and accuracy in our praise of Śrīla Prabhupāda. And I think it’s important to allow the human element to enter our praises—both our own humanity and that of Śrīla Prabhupāda. For those of us who had Prabhupāda’s personal association, we can speak about our own humanness in relation to him by telling real-life anecdotes of our exchanges with him. These anecdotes often show that we were foolish or even doubtful in our approach to the pure devotee. We were no heroes. If we didn’t personally meet Prabhupāda during his time with us, we may express our humanness by telling honestly how his *vāṇī* affected us. We should not leave out the self when reflecting on Śrīla Prabhupāda, even if it means admitting the difficulties we experienced in approaching him or the doubts we have had to face and overcome in our surrender to him. That humanness gives many others hope.

I have heard devotees say that they only want Prabhupāda in our praises of him and nothing about the praiser. Full praise, however, includes our personal participation. We want to be fully human in our approach to him and to express that, and we want to see Prabhupāda’s humanness in order to face the real person that he was.

Prabhupāda was perfect, but he was also a person with his own identity and personality. Kṛṣṇa too expresses Himself through identity and personality both when He appears in this world and when He engages in pastimes in Goloka Vṛndāvana. When Kṛṣṇa saw His friends entering the mouth of the Aghāsura demon, He showed an apparently human and frail astonishment and concern. This expression of His personality gives both Kṛṣṇa and His devotees pleasure. Similarly, when Śālva made a show of holding out Vasudeva’s head, Kṛṣṇa appeared to grieve. He was also well known as a butter thief in His childhood, and in so many ways He revealed identity and personality in His dealings with His devotees.

Prabhupāda’s humanness is one of the most appealing aspects of his personality. He revealed his humanness in his worry about the *saṅkīrtana* mission. In his Tenth Canto purports, he states that as Vasudeva was afraid that Kaṁsa would kill his children, so he was afraid that the demons could stop the *saṅkīrtana* movement. He led the charge against Māyā as a general on the battlefield heading up his immature army of devotees. Sometimes he appeared angry with us for our failings, but always he expressed his love.

Prabhupāda was full of human characteristics. He could be humorous, gentle, even whimsical at times in the way he expressed himself; he could be stern, commanding, soft, and powerful. How lovable he was in all those aspects! I must confess that I sometimes grow tired of rigid hagiography that doesn’t accommodate Prabhupāda’s humanness but emphasizes only that he was absolute or legendary. It was thrilling to be with that very human Śrīla Prabhupāda. He was such an incredible person.

I was thrilled when he called me to serve as his servant-secretary in 1973. I didn’t last more than six months, but I learned much about my spiritual master. During that time, he observed me running here and there, trying to be organized but forgetting things. He chided me in loving, instructive ways. He noticed that I had burned a ring on the floor by placing a hot pot there, and he remarked on it. He told me not to bring him his medicine, which was mixed with rice water, on Ekādaśī, but I forgot and dutifully delivered it. One day, seeing one of my discrepancies, he told me a story about Sir Isaac Newton. Sir Isaac Newton’s friend had cut two holes in Newton’s door. When another friend asked what they were for, Sir Isaac Newton said they were for his two cats to enter and exit by. “Why two holes? Why can’t the cats just go in and out of one hole?” The learned scientist replied, “I had him make them in two sizes because one of my cats is small and one big.” On hearing this, Sir Isaac’s friend laughed at the foolishness. Obviously, both cats could have gone, one at a time, through a single large-sized hole. It was double work unthinkingly done.

I think Prabhupāda told me the story to point out that I was like an absent-minded professor. At first I flattered myself, thinking that at least he had compared me to a very learned man. Perhaps Sir Isaac had been thinking of higher things and not attending to ordinary details. But I knew this couldn’t apply to me. All *my* ordinary details were parts of my personal service to the pure devotee.

When one morning, while accompanying Śrīla Prabhupāda on a morning walk, I again exhibited my absent-minded nature by locking my beads in the car, Prabhupāda said, “Sir Isaac Newton,” and walked off without me. The reference to my foolishness, spoken in an almost private language, was both mortifying and delightful.

While traveling with Prabhupāda and his party, we flew to Hawaii, Japan, and Hong Kong. Finally, we flew on to New Delhi, then headed for Vṛndāvana by car. While driving together in the car, Prabhupāda turned to me and asked whether in America we had buses like the ones we were passing. He had been to America, of course, and knew that these buses were a special specimen of Indian technological backwardness. “Even if you had such buses in America,” he added, “they would not allow them on the road.” Everyone shared a laugh with Śrīla Prabhupāda, but I took his remark personally. I felt he was trying to reduce whatever culture shock he thought I might be experiencing. In case I was lamenting over the primitive Indian conditions or wishing myself back in the land of velvetlike roads and Greyhound buses, Prabhupāda was making a humorous comparison so that I could see the terrible Indian bus in a softer, more kindly light. At least Prabhupāda’s words had that effect on me, and when he asked me if it was my first time in Vṛndāvana (which it was), I knew he was watching over me like a father. Śrīla Prabhupāda did not dote on or baby his disciples, but those few kind words were enough to bring my jet-lagged mind and body into sharp focus for appreciation of Prabhupāda and our entrance into the holy *dhāma.*

In India, I admit I became envious of the intimacy Prabhupāda extended to his leading disciples. This was another aspect of Prabhupāda’s personality: that he could associate with whomever he chose. I eventually came to understand that my advanced Godbrothers were pleasing Prabhupāda. I also came to understand that Prabhupāda accepted all of us. If we could appreciate his personal showering of mercy on those who were serving him so fearlessly, we ourselves would gain the strength to improve.

My point in telling these stories is that although they may seem like minor interludes in a legendary *ācārya’s* life, they are actually the substance of his preaching. Prabhupāda was empowered to spread the holy name all over the world, but he did not do it by remote control. He worked and traveled and cajoled and encouraged his disciples, and through his personal expressions of Kṛṣṇa conscious instruction and mercy, this transcendental *ācārya* drew out our love for him in ways that allowed us to surrender our lives. Prabhupāda did not dazzle us with mystic feats but won our hearts by his own hard work, dedication, love, and personality. We do not have to be afraid of seeing Prabhupāda as the person he was. As Śrīla Prabhupāda assures us in the *Caitanya-caritāmṛta* (*Madhya* 5.113), “By the order of the Supreme, the *nitya-siddha* remains within this material world like an ordinary man, but his only business is to broadcast the glories of the Lord. All *nitya-siddhas* within this material world may appear to toil like ordinary men, but they never forget their position as servants of the Lord.” That was Prabhupāda’s glory, that he could both appear to “toil like an ordinary man” and deliver the highest spiritual essence.

Satsvarūpa dāsa Goswami

## Śivarāma Swami

Dear Śrīla Prabhupāda,

Please accept my humble obeisances in the dust of your lotus feet.

I meditate upon those glittering particles of dust, which, by the prayers of your true followers, have received the great fortune of always cushioning your lotus feet. How fortunate they are! May I also become a grain of this dust, forever united with your divine service?

On the occasion of your Vyāsa-pūjā, I would like to narrate an incident that took place last year. Knowing your fondness for trouncing Māyāvādīs, I am confident this story will bring you some pleasure. In fact, my hope is that I may reap the reward of your wide, unrestrained, and ever-beauteous smile.

\* \* \*

In July, a well-known Hindu organization launched a worldwide *yātrā* on the grounds of Bhaktivedanta Manor. To preside over the function, they invited many spiritual leaders. To draw a crowd, they imported a popular bhajaneer. Most likely out of courtesy, they sent me an invitation.

When I saw the mīlange of spiritual dignitaries, I had second thoughts. On the stage were to be two yogis, a guru, a Śaṅkarācārya, and myself—compromising association! I inquired about the scheduled speeches and was assured I would have equal time. After consultation, I decided to make the best of the situation and participate.

Sheltering over ten thousands guests, the tent was the largest ever erected at the Manor. The guests received *prasādam,* chanted Hare Kṛṣṇa, and saw Śrī Śrī Rādhā-Gokulānanda. After all the dignitaries had arrived, the main speakers took their places onstage and the function began.

There is little value in detailing all the speeches. They included the standard fare of rambling rhetoric and humanistic clichīs, punctuated with nationalistic slogans and calls for a cultural renaissance. Oh so drab! When my turn came, I spoke for twenty minutes on the meaning of *sanātana-dharma*. I emphasized that the brotherhood of man can take place only when we accept our common father, Śrī Kṛṣṇa, and serve Him by chanting His names.

Then, sitting beside me, the Śaṅkarācārya, a small chubby man my age, with an ignoble tendency to fidget, spoke, regularly alternating between Hindi and English.

In addition to eloquent servings of mish-mash common to the other speakers, His Holiness made philosophical points clearly targeted at his hosts—namely, the ISKCON devotees and, more specifically, Their Lordships Rādhā-Gokulānanda.

When, obviously for my benefit, he said in English, “It does not matter which name you give God, for all names are temporary,” I sat erect and chanted *japa* loud enough to distract him.

In response he diligently continued to churn the mish-mash, but true to form he returned to his Māyāvāda *siddhānta* to exclaim, “The names of God are immaterial, for above name and form is the formless eternal”

I chanted aloud “Hare Kṛṣṇa Hare Kṛṣṇa” as he said, “Brahman” and my voice rang through his microphone.

When the Śaṅkarācārya snarled at me, I glared at him. As I looked into his eyes, I understood the nature of his glance. It was a glance that caught me when we first met, a glance whose description then eluded me. But that glance was a mystery no more. I understood it in one word—evil.

This is what Caitanya Mahāprabhu meant when He said *māyāvādi-bhāṣya śunile haya sarva-nāśa*. I repeated the verse under my breath as the Śaṅkarācārya faced the audience.

I continued to chant *japa* loud enough for the Śaṅkarācārya to hear. Through his speech he made one more classical jab, describing the Ultimate as pure knowledge and the worship of Deities as a temporary means to Brahman. Then it was over. Thank the Lord!

By now my pulse was pounding, and there were still speeches and *bhajans* to sit through. I felt that you, Śrīla Prabhupāda, our Society, and of course Kṛṣṇa had been grievously offended. If I said nothing I would melt in shame.

As another Yogiji began his speech, I leant over the arm of my chair and smiled, “Swamiji!” The Śaṅkarācārya turned to face me.

“I have something to say.” He nodded. “In your talk there were some highly objectionable points. They were neither śāstric nor appropriate to say on our premises,” I said.

There we were, the Śaṅkarācārya and I, in the midst of soul-sedating speeches, engaged in an animated, unamplified debate, on a stage full of dignitaries, and in plain view of thousands. Later, devotees asked me, “Were you having an interfaith dialogue?” Hardly.

The Śaṅkarācārya replied, “What I have said is all right”

“No!” I interjected smiling, “In Kṛṣṇa’s temple you have said that Kṛṣṇa is a subordinate manifestation of Brahman. You said the Absolute Truth is, in reality, *nirguṇa.* That is both impolite and against the Vedic conclusion.”

His eyes opened wide at my challenge. Then, leaning closer, the Śaṅkarācārya said, “But Kṛṣṇa says, *śāśvatasya ca dharmasya sukhasyaikāntikasya ca.* The basis of happiness is the Ultimate—Brahman.”

As Yogiji (the current speaker) was making an animated speech, I had to speak loudly. “No! You are misrepresenting Kṛṣṇa. He first says, *brahmaṇo hi pratiṣṭhāham*. He says Brahman rests on Him. If one thing rests on another it is because it is subordinate and dependent. Likewise Brahman rests on Kṛṣṇa because it is subordinate to Kṛṣṇa, and it is Kṛṣṇa who is the Ultimate.”

The Śaṅkarācārya was taken aback. It appeared he did not expect me to know the verse he had quoted. “You do not understand” he said.

I interrupted him over thunderous applause for Yogiji (who knows what he had said?). “I understand *pratiṣṭha,*” I replied. “It means Brahman *rests* on Kṛṣṇa and is *dependent* on Him. There is no ambiguity in the meaning of the word. What other understanding is there? You tell me if you know better.”

The Śaṅkarācārya was clearly flustered by my assault. He smiled me away and said, “There is no defect in Brahman. It is perfect and complete. *Oṁ pūrṇam adaḥ pūrṇam idaṁ pūrṇāt pūrṇam udacyate*”

I completed the *śloka*, again to his surprise. Then I said, “It is perfect and complete, but behind Brahman is its source and rest—a person.”

He waved his hand to dismiss my argument. I looked out over a sea of faces. The guests were alternating between hearing Yogiji, who was now singing a *bhajana,* and observing my argument with the Śaṅkarācārya. It was a double performance.

The Śaṅkarācārya rambled through textbook jargon, “Personality, form, qualities—these are all temporary features of the complete, formless Truth. The person is not behind, but produced of Brahman”

I continued, “No, the person is the basis of Brahman. *Hiraṇmayena pātreṇa satyasyāpihitaṁ mukham / tat tvaṁ pūṣann apāvṛṇu satya-dharmāya dṛṣṭaye*. *Īśopaniṣad,* which you quoted, says that Brahman should be removed to see the face, *mukham,* of the Ultimate. That *face* is the face of a person, Kṛṣṇa. One removes something inferior to reveal something superior. Thus, because Kṛṣṇa is the essence, Brahman is disposable.”

Even in this age of disposable everything, the Śaṅkarācārya was clearly disturbed to hear about the “disposable Brahman.”

Showing clear discomfort at my argument, he sidestepped it to quote (of all *śāstra*) the *Bhāgavatam* (and of all verses) “*satyaṁ paraṁ dhīmahi.*” He said, “Swamiji, Brahman is knowledge, truth—*satyam*—and it is Supreme—*param*.”

I was really disappointed at the Śaṅkarācārya’s repertoire of half-done *ślokas*. I fumed, “But who is *paraṁ satyam*? Vyāsadeva prays, *oṁ namo bhagavate vāsudevāya*. It is Vāsudeva, Kṛṣṇa. So meditate on Him, for He is the Supreme Truth. That is what *paraṁ satyam* means. *Paraṁ satyam*, the object of meditation (*dhīmahi*) at the end of the *śloka,* is the same *bhāgavate vāsudevāya* offered respects (*namaḥ*) at its beginning. That is Jaimini’s law.”

A round of applause for Yogiji’s completed *bhajana* filled the air, as my opponent abandoned *śāstra* for incongruous logic. Shaking with anger, his eyebrows furrowed, the Śaṅkarācārya said, “It is unnecessary to argue over *apparent* differences.”

Saying this he raised his arm and pointed to one of the overhead lights at stage right. As he did, the entire audience followed his gesture. Even Yogiji looked distracted as he continued to propound the glories of the motherland, the mother tongue, and one’s own mother.

The Śaṅkarācārya said, “That light is energy. But it is the energy of a flowing river now transformed into electricity. In the ultimate issue they are one. Everywhere there is one energy, which appears in different forms. Similarly Kṛṣṇa”

Having no taste for what was coming, I cut in, “But who designed the machinery which transforms the energy of a river into the energy of light?” I slapped my armrest to the surprise of some dignitaries in the first row. “They were engineers—people. People manipulate energy, not visa versa. Thus, in your example, Kṛṣṇa, the Supreme Person, manipulates Brahman to be the substratum of energy by which He creates and pervades this world, *jagat.*”

Much to the Śaṅkarācārya’s dismay, I continued to argue the example of the stage light. When I pointed to a spotlight at stage left, the audience turned right; in his retort the Śaṅkarācārya pointed to a spotlight at stage right, and the audience turned left. And so it went on.

Finally Yogiji finished his talk, and both I and the Śaṅkarācārya settled back in our seats. But there was one last thing to say. I smiled at him, “But Swamiji, you know what Ādi Śaṅkara said.”

While the MC was extolling the glories of the next speaker, the Śaṅkarācārya replied gruffly, “What is that?”

I said, “*bhaja govindaṁ bhaja govindaṁ bhaja govindam,*” and stopped.

He looked at me, and for a moment our eyes locked. It was more than the conflict of two men, more than a conflict of different schools of philosophy. It was the age-old clash of two classes of souls: those who try to justify their rebellion against Kṛṣṇa, and those who desire to rectify it.

Gazing into his eyes, speaking very audibly, I continued, “*mūḍha-mate.*”

The Śaṅkarācārya jerked—shocked. In a public assembly I had implied he was a *mūḍha*!

To soften the blow I continued, “*prāpte sannihite kāla maraṇe na hi na hi rakṣati ḍukṛñ-karaṇe*.”

He turned away. But I said nothing.

Perhaps I was guilty of inhospitality to a guest—an unfortunate transgression of etiquette. However, I did not want to tolerate grievous offenses to the Lord—a major spiritual transgression.

Sure enough, in a moment the Śaṅkarācārya leant over to me and said, “Excuse me, I must go to the toilet.” He rose with his escort of followers and reassured me, “I will be back. I will be back.”

But I knew he wouldn’t be back. The organizers were unsure why the Śaṅkarācārya left untimely in his Mercedes, retinue and all. They were puzzled, I was happy.

\* \* \*

Dear Śrīla Prabhupāda! The dialogue I have given above is accurate. For obvious reasons I have avoided mentioning names.

Thank you, Śrīla Prabhupāda, for being the perfect teacher. You have taught us the perfect science of transcendence, you have taught us the arguments of those who oppose that science, and you have taught us how to refute those arguments.

While speaking to the Śaṅkarācārya, I felt so proud to be your disciple. I felt so confident that nothing he said could shake my faith. I knew that for every misconception he might put forward, you have given us a perfect retort. For you, the Absolute Truth was not a philosophical concept but your personal friend and loving associate.

That night, after the speeches had concluded, after the guests had departed, after the dignitaries had been dined, I sat before my Deities to review the debate with the Śaṅkarācārya. As you once said, “It was a good fight.” I looked at your picture: there was no doubt, you were pleased. I reveled in that wonderful feeling of absolute certainty, “Today I have pleased my spiritual master.”

May it always be so.

*Your servant,*  
Śivarāma Swami

## Tamāl Krishna Goswami

*On March 15, 2002, His Holiness Tamāl Krishna Goswami passed away in an automobile accident near Māyāpur, India. One of Śrīla Prabhupāda’s most senior and beloved disciples, in 1984 he had published a memoir entitled* Servant of the Servant, *describing his early years in the Hare Kṛṣṇa movement and his relationship with Śrīla Prabhupāda. Here we present an excerpt in which he describes the first lectures he heard Śrīla Prabhupāda give, in San Francisco during the spring of 1968.*

Śrīla Prabhupāda’s exposition of the Kṛṣṇa conscious philosophy was not at all like the lectures of the “Western *guru”* we had been attending. Prabhupāda began the Friday evening lecture by reading in Sanskrit from a large book, from which he continued to draw references throughout his talk. Although to me the Sanskrit was incomprehensible, I was impressed with its authoritative sound. It was clear that Śrīla Prabhupāda was expounding an ancient philosophy. His constant references to the text indicated that he was not speculating, trying to create his own brand of philosophy. And very clearly his points about how we were all suffering in this material world made sense. Kṛṣṇa consciousness, he said, would solve all problems of material existence. The easy method of chanting Hare Kṛṣṇa would raise us to the spiritual platform, beyond material happiness and distress. Each one of us, he explained, could practically experience the transcendental effect by chanting Hare Kṛṣṇa. Yes, I had experienced this.

The lecture was deep, and at times beyond my comprehension. But much that was said was common sense, and I could relate it to my own life. I felt that Prabhupāda’s talk was directed at actually trying to benefit his audience. He was not trying to impress anyone by boasting of his own realizations, though it was clear he was speaking from personal conviction. There was no need for him to advertise himself, because he was not calling for followers but rather was appealing to the audience to try to improve their present condition and end their suffering way of life. It was practical.

Although I had attended numerous lectures by \[the Western guru\], they consisted mostly of abstract ideas presented in a contrived, complex terminology which I found difficult to remember from one meeting to the next. The meditation sessions had been no less confusing. Each person was encouraged to “travel” out of his body, and upon reentering, to describe where he or she had been. There was no direction given as to how to channel our energy toward a particular goal. Whatever the goal was, \[the Western guru\] had never clearly defined it. Comparing the two, I felt that there was more to gain by hearing Prabhupāda lecture. Chanting Hare Kṛṣṇa was a more tangible process than some vague meditation.

I began to attend all of the evening lectures at the temple. As I became familiar with the terms Prabhupāda used, the whole subject matter became more comprehensible. I purchased a set of *Śrīmad-Bhāgavatams.* Reading them each day confirmed what Prabhupāda was speaking in his lectures.

In each of his lectures Prabhupāda stressed the importance of avoiding sinful activities, particularly intoxication, meat-eating, illicit sex, and gambling. It was the first time I had ever heard anyone explain that such behavior would impair spiritual development. Śrīla Prabhupāda was not compromising the absolute truth. He had come to deliver the timeless teachings of the spiritual world as he had heard them from the disciplic succession, and there was nothing to be gained by watering down the process simply to increase the number of his followers. He was not a cheater, like the other *gurus* I had seen or heard of. He had full faith that Kṛṣṇa consciousness could deliver anyone who was sincere. Hearing only one lecture gave me the strength to avoid all of the sinful activities I had been habituated to for years. In Prabhupāda’s straightforward presentation of the four regulative principles, I found the proper direction I needed.

## Vyāsa-pūjā Homages from Sannyāsīs

## Bhakti Balabh Purī Goswami

*oṁ ajñāna-timirāndhasya jñānāñjana-śalākayā*  
*cakṣur unmīlitaṁ yena tasmai śrī-gurave namaḥ*

*nama oṁ viṣṇu-pādāya kṛṣṇa-preṣṭhāya bhū-tale*  
*śrīmate bhaktivedānta-svāminn iti nāmine*

*namas te sārasvate deve gaura-vāṇī-pracāriṇe*  
*nirviśeṣa-śūnyavādi-pāścātya-deśa-tāriṇe*

Dear Śrīla Prabhupāda,

Please accept my respectful obeisances at your lotus feet. All glories to you and your great mercy, which is spreading throughout the entire planet!

What a gift you have given the world! Or should it factually be said, What gifts you have given the world! The gift of *kṛṣṇa-nāma*! What other gift can compare? You taught us that everything is contained within the name, that everything is coming from the name, that the name and Kṛṣṇa are one and the same. You have given Kṛṣṇa to the world. You also gave the world Śrī Śrī Gaura-Nitāi, to make sure that all the fallen souls would certainly attain love of Kṛṣṇa. You, Śrīla Prabhupāda, are the real mercy incarnation of the Lord. To see the holy names spread to every *jīva* on the planet is a very exciting thought and task. This is the service you have left for us, “the children of mercy.” We are trying to see that the name does go out throughout the world. We have recently started an Internet program that delivers the holy names to as many e-mail addresses as possible. We are targeting different communities in different parts of the world. At present we are concentrating on the Jews in Israel. Next we will concentrate on all Muslims who have e-mail addresses—first those in the Middle East, then all others throughout the world. In one evening we mailed out the holy names to over 60,000 people. We are hoping to continue with this program as much as possible and take advantage of the world’s present technology to distribute your priceless gift to so many in the world as quickly as possible.

Thank you for this engagement and all others you have given me. Without your mercy I would be one of the people out there waiting for the e-mail of the holy names to come in over the Internet.

I thank you once again for the association of your dedicated devotees, and the service to my Deities.

I am sorry that I have not been much use in your mission, but by your mercy I am part of it, although a very small and insignificant part. Thank you.

I am sure that just by being in your mission, at some point in my future *kṛṣṇa-prema* will be mine. Your mission is engaging us in our eternal constitutional activity—serving our Lord. Your mercy!

All glories to all your disciples who are doing so much to push forward your mission and are pleasing you greatly!

All glories to all the souls you have engaged in your mission, even if they do not appear to be serving presently. They will return, as you said. All glories to those souls yet to come and serve your mission. They are all the most fortunate of souls, for they are going to receive your mercy. Your mercy, that which transports the soul form hell to Vaikuṇṭha. That which transforms envy into *kṛṣṇa-prema*. That which has changed the existence of all who come together throughout the world today to offer glorification to you for what you have given and are still giving in abundance—*kṛṣṇa-prema—*through the holy name, your books, and simple service.

All glories to you, Śrīla Prabhupāda, my Lord and master! I apologize for my offenses and laziness, and I thank you for keeping this fool at your feet. Don’t let me go. Please.

Śrīla Prabhupāda *kī jaya*!  
Śrī Hari-nāma Prabhu *kī jaya*!

*Your insignificant servant, the worthless,*  
Bhakti Balabh Purī Goswami (the fool you named Balabhadra dāsa—no poet, no philosopher, no use, but yours and honored to be so)

## Bhakti Rāghava Swami

*oṁ ajñāna-timirāndhasya jñānāñjana-śalākayā*  
*cakṣur unmīlitaṁ yena tasmai śrī-gurave namaḥ*

**I was born in the darkness of ignorance, and my spiritual master opened my eyes with the torchlight of knowledge. I bow down before him and offer my respectful obeisances.**

*śrī-caitanya-mano-’bhīṣṭaṁ sthāpitaṁ yena bhū-tale*  
*svayaṁ rūpaḥ kadā mahyaṁ dadāti sva-padāntikam*

**When will Śrīla Rūpa Gosvāmī Prabhupāda, who has established within this material world the mission to fulfill the desire of Lord Caitanya, give me shelter under his lotus feet?**

My dear Śrīla Prabhupāda,

Please accept my most humble obeisances at your lotus feet. All glories to you, Śrīla Prabhupāda, and all glories to your continuing glorious transcendental preaching activities!

Practically every day I write so many letters to different devotees around the world, but it is only once a year that I write you a personal letter. I cannot adequately express my feelings of gratitude for what you have done, not only for me but for countless other living entities, by giving us the gift of Kṛṣṇa consciousness and the privilege of pure devotional service.

Although I write to you only once a year, every day I hear you chanting *japa* in the early morning hours, every day I read some verse and your purport from the *Śrīmad-Bhāgavatam* and *Bhagavad-gītā*, every day I offer you a small flower, and every day I place a small garland around your neck and take *darśana* of your holy feet. You remain a constant source of inspiration for me and, I know, for thousands of devotees around the world.

Your purports remain so clear and so illuminating. In the Second Canto of *Śrīmad-Bhāgavatam*, Chapter Nine, entitled “Answers by Citing the Lord’s Version,” you write in your purport to text 40:

One cannot be situated in an exalted position without having undertaken a regulative life of rules and regulations. An unrestricted life of sense gratification is animal life, and Lord Brahmā, in order to teach all concerned within the jurisdiction of his generations, taught the same principles of sense control for executing higher duties. He desired the welfare of all as servants of God, and anyone desiring the welfare of the members of his family and generations must conduct a moral, religious life. The highest life of moral principles is to become a devotee of the Lord because a pure devotee of the Lord has all the good qualities of the Lord. On the other hand, one who is not a devotee of the Lord, however qualified he may be in the mundane sense of the term, cannot be qualified with any good quality worthy of the name. The pure devotees of the Lord, like Brahmā and persons in the chain of disciplic succession, do not do anything to instruct their subordinates without acting accordingly themselves.

You are such a pure representative of the Lord, and your only desire is to bring that highest welfare, devotional service, to as many living entities as possible. I simply pray I can assist you in some menial way. I am convinced that my only business in life is to continue engaging in your devotional service, although my efforts remain tiny and insignificant. Without your constant guidance and causeless mercy, I would simply be lost in this ocean of material suffering. You are my savior and my only hope.

You understood perfectly well the perils of today’s modern society, the great “Western adventure,” you would call it. In your perfect vision and wisdom, similar to that of Śrīla Bhaktivinoda Ṭhākura, who predicted that devotees from different countries would one day congregate in Śrī Dhāma Māyāpur, you have also predicted the downfall of the present demoniac civilization.

Daily we see increasing signs of the deterioration and degradation of society, where the finer values in human life are being challenged, abused, and rejected. Daily we witness the onslaught of modern technology and modern speculative ideologies tearing away at the lives of human beings, animals, and mother nature herself. You warned us and even urged us to become separate and independent from the present demoniac civilization by establishing *varṇāśrama*. This was and remains one of your unfulfilled dreams and desires, expressed especially during the last year of your physical presence with us. I pray we can move much more quickly and more decisively in this direction.

Your ISKCON continues to survive although threatened from both inside and outside. The challenges before us are many and grave. At times it becomes almost bewildering to know how we can overcome the various obstacles we face. In the past, many devotees of the Lord faced similar obstacles. Yet they remained steadfast in their determination to serve Lord Kṛṣṇa by taking shelter of His instructions and devotees. May we likewise take shelter of your lotus feet and your instructions.

I ask your kind forgiveness for all my shortcomings and mistakes. I pray that you will remain merciful to this fallen soul, who desires to remain attached to serving your instructions. By serving your instructions I know I am serving you directly, and by serving you now I know I will one day join you wherever you may be.

Thank you again, Śrīla Prabhupāda, for the wonderful gift of Kṛṣṇa consciousness, without which I would simply be lost and forsaken. May your glories be spread more and more all over the three worlds.

*Your servant,*  
Bhakti Rāghava Swami

## Bhakti Vighna Vināśa Narasiṁha Mahārāja

My dearmost spiritual master and eternal father,

Please accept my most humble obeisances at your lotus feet.

I am finding the pains of this past year to be more than any in recent memory. The cause is not the usual attacks of the atheists, materialists, Māyāvādīs, or *apasampradāyas,* but it is brought on by the temporary nature of our mortal frames and the resulting loss of two of your loving servants, Tribhuvanātha Prabhu and George Harrison. I cannot claim to have had an intimate relationship with either of them, but I recognize that they both had a lot to do with my involvement in the Kṛṣṇa consciousness movement. I am only one of hundreds of thousands if not millions who contacted you and the Kṛṣṇa consciousness movement by their efforts to assist you in Lord Caitanya’s *saṅkīrtana* movement. Tribhuvanātha was the temple manager when I first came to the temple, and he allowed me to stay overnight and attend the morning program. Although he was still in his teens, he was the driving force of the temple, enlivening the devotees with his ecstatic *kīrtanas* and convincing preaching. However, even before I met Tribhuvanātha I had become attracted to Kṛṣṇa consciousness through the music of the Rādhā Kṛṣṇa Temple Band and their recordings of “The Hare Kṛṣṇa Mantra” and “Govinda,” both of which were produced by George Harrison. Then I purchased a *Kṛṣṇa* book, which had been printed by his donation. The book had such an impact on me that I had to visit the temple, and thus I gained the association of your devotees.

I owe a big debt to Tribhuvanātha, George, and all the devotees of the Kṛṣṇa consciousness movement who kindly tolerate my presence and allow me to participate in your movement. I owe an especially huge debt to you, my eternal spiritual guide and father, because it was you who planted the seed of *bhakti* in their hearts and it was your perfect example of pure devotion to Śrī Kṛṣṇa that convinced them the goal of life is service to His lotus feet. It is said that a Vaiṣṇava is known by how many others he can bring to Kṛṣṇa consciousness. Just as Śrī Caitanya Mahāprabhu brought many important people of his time to devotional service, in the same way you also awakened devotion in some very special people who would assist you in your mission.

I would also like to do some nice service for your pleasure, but as my years increase I become more aware of my frailties in this matter. I therefore pray that I might remain a foot soldier in your service, and as Lord Rāmacandra recognized the efforts of a tiny but devoted spider who helped Him construct the bridge to Laṅkā, in the same way I hope you will bless my attempts to serve you and keep me engaged in your service.

*Your insignificant servant,*  
Bhakti Vighna Vināśa Narasiṁha Mahārāja

## Bhakti Vikāsa Swami

*nama oṁ viṣṇu-pādāya kṛṣṇa-preṣṭhāya bhū-tale*  
*śrīmate bhaktivedānta-svāminn iti nāmine*

*namas te sārasvate deve gaura-vāṇī-pracāriṇe*  
*nirviśeṣa-śūnyavādi-pāścātya-deśa-tāriṇe*

Śrīla Prabhupāda, I’m not as outgoing or dynamic as I could be. In a world that needs Kṛṣṇa now, I spend considerable time sitting—reading, writing, and reflecting. You described that you had built the “skyscraper framework” of the Kṛṣṇa consciousness movement and that we, your disciples, should fill it in. But to do that effectively requires more than merely supplying building materials. Even if the framework is firm, to affix poor-quality bricks or add good-quality bricks without having proper knowledge of construction may result in a structure that, albeit large, is yet dysfunctional and possibly even dangerous, rather than a facility in which the whole world can live safely and securely.

Considering this, I have been investigating the nature of the materials that build the house of *bhakti* in this world. With your teachings as the basis, I am researching and gradually writing books on the principles and details of *varṇāsrama-dharma, sādhana-bhakti,* Vaiṣṇava behavior, guru-disciple relationships, and other important topics. Obliged to teach others, on your order I also travel, preaching what I have learned from you, as you have taught us to do.

Śrīla Prabhupāda, please help me gain realization of how to appropriately apply your instructions in this complex world. Please bless me with good intelligence and competent assistance so that I may quickly complete these writing projects. Moreover, please bless me with a long life so that I may help in practically demonstrating these concepts in action, thereby further bringing to fruition your dream of a Kṛṣṇa conscious world.

Ever desiring your service, I remain  
*Your apprentice,*  
Bhakti Vikāsa Swami

## Bhakti Viśrambha Mādhava Svāmī

*vṛndāvane ramaṇa-reti prasiddha-bhūmau*  
*tatrāpi kṛṣṇa-balarāma-supāda-mūle*  
*jñānaṁ paraṁ parama-kṛṣṇa-sudharmīty uktaṁ*  
*daṇḍas tu deva prabhupāda namo namas te*

**In the transcendental land of Vṛndāvana’s pleasing Ramaṇa-reti sands, situated at the lotus feet of Kṛṣṇa-Balarāma, Śrīla Prabhupāda is delivering the highest knowledge about Kṛṣṇa. I repeatedly offer my respectful obeisances unto that lord at whose lotus feet all the prabhus sit.**

(Composed in 1975 by Paṇḍit Ananta Śāstrī, the Sanskrit professor at the Kṛṣṇa-Balarāma Gurukula)

Śrīla Prabhupāda, in a conversation in 1975 you said, “We are presenting everything: God’s name, His father’s name, His residence, address—everything is given. Why is there any need for researching God?”

All glories to you, Śrīla Prabhupāda! Out of your causeless mercy upon all the fallen conditioned souls of this world, like me, you have written the *Kṛṣṇa* book to attract us all back home to Godhead. After reading it in two weeks at the impressionable age of fifteen, in January 1972, by your grace I intuitively came to one conclusion—that human life is meant for becoming pure and playing with Kṛṣṇa.

I really wanted then, and still only want, to serve Kṛṣṇa by giving Him pleasure by sporting and playing in His company with the other cowherd boys. That ecstatic position is not so easily attained, except by the mercy won by following those who are situated in that position, as you are, Śrīla Prabhupāda.

Your disciple Mahākratu Prabhu told me he saw you eagerly describe this perfection while walking with some of your disciples in Nairobi’s city park in 1975. You saw the nice British bandstand in the flower-decorated park. “Oh, in such a similar round *maṇḍala* Kṛṣṇa-Balarāma would sit with their friends and eat lunch. The perfection of life—to eat lunch with Kṛṣṇa-Balarāma.”

Śrīla Prabhupāda, your absorption in the glorification and service of Kṛṣṇa-Balarāma is so crystal clear. You established Their temple in Ramaṇa-reti in 1975. When Gaura Govinda Swami informed you that the sand on the land for a planned ISKCON temple in Bhubaneshwar was similar to that at Ramaṇa-reti, you said, “Oh, then it must be a Kṛṣṇa-Balarāma temple.”

To a man in Jaipura you wrote:

You are Jaipuria, so you can help me in completing such an institution in Jaipur also. Jaipur is visited by many Vaisnavas as a holy place of pilgrimage. Especially Gaudiya Vaisnavas go there to see the duplicate Vrindaban Deities such as Govindaji, Radha Damodaraji, etc. So I want to open a duplicate Krishna Balarama temple in Jaipur. I am sending separately copies of pictures of our Krishna Balarama temple in Vrindaban. Of course there is also Radha Krishna and Gaura Nitai. I wish that you have some land in Jaipur and to help us in constructing a duplicate temple like Vrindaban. \[letter to Mahavir Prasad Jaipuriaji, 13 July 1975\]

Thus you also desired your Kṛṣṇa-Balarāma to have a nice temple in Jaipura, in Rajasthan, India. This past November 25, 2001, in the company of Gopāl Krishna Goswami and Jayapatāka Swami, I was pleased to witness the devotional efforts of the Jaipura ISKCON devotees, headed by Rāmānuja and Pañcaratna Prabhus, who have strived to fulfill your desire by installing Kṛṣṇa-Balarāma Deities named Giridhārī-Doujī. They look strikingly similar to your Kṛṣṇa-Balarāma Deities at the New Govardhana farm community in Murwillumbah, Australia.

One of your favorite verses is *Śrīmad-Bhāgavatam* 10.12.11:

*itthaṁ satāṁ brahma-sukhānubhūtyā*  
*dāsyaṁ gatānāṁ para-daivatena*  
*māyāśritānāṁ nara-dārakeṇa*  
*sārdhaṁ vijahruḥ kṛta-puṇya-puñjāḥ*

**“Neither those engaged in the self-realization of appreciating the Brahman effulgence of the Lord, nor those engaged in devotional service while accepting the Supreme Personality of Godhead as master, nor those under the clutches of Māyā, thinking the Lord an ordinary person, can understand that certain exalted personalities, after accumulating volumes of pious activities, are now playing with the Lord in friendship as cowherd boys.’”**

I understood when I first read your *Kṛṣṇa* books, as I do now, that to get the qualifications necessary to deserve such a position it is essential to *first* become pure by accepting the necessary training under you and your pure servants. This training, as you aptly put it, involves “spending gallons of blood” and is one of the essential purposes of your ISKCON. That training involves a demonstrable and responsible devotional accountability in interpersonal dealings with all the strata of your devotees—seniors, peers, and subordinates.

Śrīla Prabhupāda, in the days after your *Kṛṣṇa* book propelled me to move toward joining your ISKCON in 1973, you gave me shelter and training under Tamāl Krishna Goswami and Viṣṇujana Swami. Regrettably, due to distractions, I did not take full advantage of such senior association in those heady years of Rādhā-Dāmodara TSKP.

Since 1996, however, I’ve had the good fortune to experience your continuing presence and blessings in the form of the inspiring association and example of your pure generals Nirañjana and Rādhānātha Swamis, along with Bhūrijana and Gopīparāṇadhana Prabhus.

Training means voluntarily accepting devotionally-minded *tapasya*. In Vṛndāvana, on September 9, 1975, while lecturing on *Śrīmad-Bhāgavatam* 6.2.17, you put the onus of applying your desires for systematic training on all your followers: “Without *tapasya* you cannot be purified. That little *tapasya* we have prescribed, that ‘Rise early in the morning at half past three,’ but they are so downtrodden, they cannot do it.”

Those training efforts applied in a pure, intensive daily program of *sādhana-bhakti* are the source of all the spiritual strength for all our full-time and congregational, part-time devotees. Nowadays we see that many of our congregational devotees and former full-time members are forced by many extenuating circumstances to work outside our temples; unfortunately, we see that devotees who, for whatever reason, compromise their *sādhana* for fleeting gain are not happy at all.

Your preaching is compelled by your universal compassion—you wanted everyone to be happy by serving Kṛṣṇa. You instructed us to not try to *see* Kṛṣṇa—an effort that would not make us happy or liberate us—but to try to *hear* Kṛṣṇa, i.e., to follow His instructions with a submissive service attitude. One can acquire and reliably sustain that service attitude only by engaging more and more in quality hearing and chanting about Kṛṣṇa.

You were so concerned about proper training for all your devotees. You wrote to your disciples first in 1969 and later in 1976, insisting that a systematic training program be instituted in ISKCON.

One Rupanuga Vidyapitha—that is a school for educating brahmanas and Vaisnavas. We have enough of technological and other types of educational institutions, but perhaps there is none where actual brahmanas and Vaisnavas are produced. So we will have to establish an educational institution for that purpose.

A first examination will be held sometimes next January on Bhagavad-gita As It Is, and those passing will have the degree of Bhakti-sastri. Next year we will hold an examination on Srimad-Bhagavatam, and the person who passes will have the title Bhakti-vaibhava. And the next year we shall hold an examination on Teachings of Lord Caitanya, Nectar of Devotion and Vedanta Sutra, and those who will successfully pass will be awarded with the title of Bhaktivedanta. By 1975, all of those who have passed all of the above examinations will be specifically empowered to initiate and increase the number of the Krishna Consciousness population. \[Letter to Kīrtanānanda, 12 January 1969\]

\* \* \*

To all Governing Body Commissioners

Re: Examinations for awarding titles of Bhakti-sastri, Bhakti-vaibhava, Bhaktivedanta and Bhakti-sarvabhauma. Your response is requested immediately by Srila Prabhupada.

Dear Prabhus,

Please accept my most humble obeisances. Srila Prabhupada has requested me to write you in regard to the above examinations which he wishes to institute. Here in India many persons often criticize our sannyasis and brahmanas as being unqualified due to insufficient knowledge of the scriptures. Factually, there are numerous instances when our sannyasis and brahmanas have fallen down often due to insufficient understanding of the philosophy. This should not be a point of criticism nor a reason for falldown, since Srila Prabhupada has mercifully made the most essential scriptures available to us in his books. The problem is that not all the devotees are carefully studying the books, the result being a fall down or at least unsteadiness.

His Divine Grace therefore wishes to institute examinations to be given to all prospective candidates for sannyasa and brahmana initiation. In addition he wishes that all present sannyasis and brahmanas also pass the examination. Awarding of these titles will be based upon the following books:

Bhakti-sastri—Bhagavad-gita, Nectar of Devotion, Nectar of Instruction, Isopanisad, Easy Journey To Other Planets, and all other small paperbacks, as well as Arcana-paddhati (a book to be compiled by Nitai Prabhu based on Hari-bhakti-vilasa on Deity worship)

Bhakti-vaibhava—All of the above plus the first six cantos of Srimad-Bhagavatam

Bhaktivedanta—All of the above plus cantos 7 through 12 of Srimad-Bhagavatam

Bhakti-sarvabhauma—All the above plus the entire Caitanya-caritamrta

Anyone wishing to be initiated as a brahmana will have to pass the Bhaktisastri exam and anyone wishing to take sannyasa will have to pass the Bhaktivaibhava examination as well. This will prevent our Society from degrading to the level of so many other institutions where, in order to maintain the Temple, they accept all third class men as brahmanas. Any sannyasis or brahmanas already initiated who fail to pass the exams will be considered low class or less qualified. Anyone wishing to be 2nd initiated will sit for examination once a year at Mayapur. Answers will be in essay form and authoritative quotations will be given a bigger score. During the exams books may not be consulted.

Srila Prabhupada wishes to begin this program at this year’s Mayapur meeting. He requests that you all send your opinions and comments here immediately so that everything may be prepared in time.

Hoping this meets you in the best of health and Krsna Consciousness.

Your servant,  
Tamala Krsna Goswami  
Personal Secretary  
Approved: A. C. Bhaktivedanta Swami  
\[Letter to Akṣayānanda Swami, 6 January 1976\]

\* \* \*

Bhakti-sastri, Bhakti-vaibhava, Bhaktivedanta, and Bhakti-sarvabhauma. All our brahmanas and anyone wanting to become brahmana will have to sit for examination once a year at Mayapur. They will be expected to know Bhagavad-gita, Nectar of Devotion, Nectar of Instruction, Sri Isopanisad, a book soon to be published on Deity worship, as well as all the small paperbacks. If they pass the examination they will be awarded Bhakti-sastri certificate. Sannyasis will be asked to sit for Bhakti-vaibhava examination which will include the first six cantos of Bhagavatam.

In our Vedic Universities we will not encourage anyone to be merely a bookworm. There must be life—rising early in the morning, attending mangala arati, taking prasadam, etc. The man who is studying will be brahmana, the farmer will be vaisya. In this way there will be divisions, but they are all one in service to Krishna.

I hope this meets you in the best of health.

Your ever well-wisher,  
A. C. Bhaktivedanta Swami  
\[Letter to Tusta Krsna dāsa, 9 January 1976\]

\* \* \*

Because you wanted your senior devotees responsible for front-line preaching to remain pure and absorbed genuinely in *śāstra,* especially those who served as *sannyāsīs,* you requested them in your letters to submit to examinations to judge how well they’d assimilated the teachings.

To begin to systematically fulfill this desire of yours, Śrīla Prabhupāda, this year ISKCON is holding the first Bhaktivaibhava seminars in Vṛndāvana.

Śrīla Prabhupāda, you eternally play with Kṛṣṇa-Balarāma and also serve the divine couple Rādhā-Kṛṣṇa’s intimate pastimes, and when you were physically with us you showed the characteristics of one worthy of engaging in such divine service. The chief of those characteristics was your unswerving dedication to the preaching mission of your Guru Mahārāja, which you showed by preaching tirelessly from a platform of purity.

As we struggle to follow in your preaching footsteps twenty-five years after your physical departure, it is becoming increasingly clear that no devotee, however gifted, can get the strength to learn, assimilate, and apply your teachings without your divine grace.

Please give me your blessings and the strength to apply your instructions and desires in my life. Only in this way will I hope to satisfy you and increase your happiness. If one can achieve *niṣṭha-bhakti,* or steadiness, by adopting the recommended processes—as you nicely said, “first deserve, then desire”—then one can aspire to be able to please you.

Oh, when will that day come when you’ll be pleased to take this fallen one into your company again? Oh, when will you smile at me and take my trembling hand in yours? When will I be able to run and run to touch Kṛṣṇa first and obtain the goal of playing in the company of Kṛṣṇa and his friends in his Bhauma-Vṛndāvana pastimes, relishing as you eternally relish?

In hopes of the ultimate goal of life, I must now and perpetually rededicate myself to the path of purity by following in your footsteps, accepting the shelter of your dedicated generals among your disciples and grand-disciples in ISKCON, and submitting to ongoing training and devotional accountability under them.

Seeking and surviving by your and their blessings, I remain,

*Your fallen servant,*  
Bhakti Viśrambha Mādhava Svāmī

## Candramauli Swami

Dear Śrīla Prabhupāda,

*nama oṁ viṣṇu-pādāya kṛṣṇa-preṣṭhāya bhū-tale*  
*śrīmate bhaktivedānta-svāminn iti nāmine*

*namas te sārasvate deve gaura-vāṇī-pracāriṇe*  
*nirviśeṣa-śūnyavādi-pāścātya-deśa-tāriṇe*

Please accept my repeated prostrated obeisances in the dust of your lotus feet.

On this most auspicious and glorious occasion of the anniversary of your divine appearance in the world, we humbly attempt to offer a few words that will inspire us in our service and remind us of your mercy upon the fallen souls.

In the *Bhagavad-gītā* (2.41) Lord Kṛṣṇa speaks to Śrī Arjuna on the importance of one-pointed determination for the goal.

Those who are on this path are resolute in purpose, and their aim is one. O beloved child of the Kurus, the intelligence of those who are irresolute is many-branched.

Śrīla Prabhupāda, you told us that in the 1950’s you read a commentary on this verse by Śrīla Viśvanātha Cakravartī Ṭhākura that strengthened your resolve to follow Śrīla Bhaktisiddhānta Sarasvatī Ṭhākura’s order to preach Kṛṣṇa consciousness to the English-speaking world.

Śrīla Viśvanātha Cakravartī Ṭhākura states:

Of all the intelligence, the best is intelligence focused on *bhakti-yoga*. In *bhakti-yoga,* one’s intelligence becomes single-pointed, or fixed in determination. One thinks, “The instructions of my spiritual master to worship the Personality of Godhead by chanting, remembering, serving His feet, and so on are my only *sādhana,* my only *sādhya,* my only livelihood. I am unable to give up these instructions either in the stage of practice or in the stage of perfection. They alone are my object of desire and my only responsibility. Besides them I can desire no other responsibility, not even in my dreams. It is all the same to me whether I feel happy or unhappy, or whether my material existence is eradicated or not.

These words by Viśvanātha Cakravartī Ṭhākura remind us of what you expected from your disciples and followers in ISKCON.

We cannot be satisfied simply by our own spiritual progress. In fact, to receive your mercy we must try to reach out to others in a mood of compassion. This you stress again and again throughout your writings—that the goal of this movement and the responsibility of its members is to preach Kṛṣṇa consciousness as widely as possible. So it is clear that our service should be executed in such a way that others will be inspired and somehow or other attracted to take up spiritual life.

In the *Bhagavad-gītā* (13.8–12), Lord Śrī Kṛṣṇa lists the items of knowledge. The definition of actual nonviolence you give in your purport is astounding, giving deeper meaning that reflects the purpose of this Kṛṣṇa Consciousness movement.

Nonviolence is generally taken to mean not killing or destroying the body, but actually nonviolence means not to put others into distress. People in general are trapped by ignorance in the material concept of life, and they perpetually suffer material pains. So unless one elevates people to spiritual knowledge, one is practicing violence. One should try his best to distribute real knowledge to the people, so that they may become enlightened and leave this material entanglement. That is nonviolence.

In your purport to *Śrīmad-Bhāgavatam* 3.21.31, you stress the importance of distributing Kṛṣṇa consciousness to others, describing it as the responsibility of everyone who wants to seriously execute devotional service.
